Is it Disney who gets to be the angriest, or is it Drake, or is it someone in the middle? Can you help me just organize my thoughts here?

First of all, I’m so glad you’re bringing this up because we literally, over the past three years, have been dealing with exactly this. Because believe it or not, and it’s so bizarre to think about, none of us right now has a right to our own voice or likeness under federal law. Most of us don’t even have it under state law. State law, which is called the right of publicity, usually only applies to advertising or endorsements. I couldn’t take your face and put it on a Wheaties box without asking you because I’m selling it and making money, and I’d be using your popularity to do so. Those are very limited rights. Now we’re in the world of deep fakes where everybody can do anything, make you say or do something you never said, you never did, and that you might not approve of — and we don’t have a basic right to ourselves under the law.

It seems so weird in a country that was founded on individual liberty and individual rights that we wouldn’t have an individual right to ourselves. So we had to sort of start from scratch. And what we did was get together with YouTube in the very beginning. These things, even if they’re not created by the platforms, the avenue to the fan is through the platform. And everybody’s not going to bring a lawsuit against everybody in order to get this stuff down when it’s proliferating as fast as it is. So the question was, what responsibility does or can the platform have to give a very simple takedown mechanism that anybody could use? Because this is your face, it’s your right. It’s not like the record company owns the right to your face, or the movie company. You own the right to your face.

So if you don’t like the fact that somebody used your face or your voice in a way you didn’t like, you should be able to very quickly say, “That’s me, I can prove it’s me, and I don’t want it up there.” And the platform should want to do something to protect the integrity of the platform without liability.

Can I just stop you? This is why it’s a nightmare hypothetical, right? A longstanding trope at The Verge started by our former colleague, Sarah Jeong, is that copyright law is the only functional law on the internet. The only way you actually really want to regulate the internet is to take things down. That is the thing that people want to accomplish. The First Amendment basically prohibits the government from creating some mechanism to take things down, except in the case of copyright law, where we have the Digital Millennium Copyright Act. There’s a huge notice-and-takedown regime in that law, and it is frequently abused on all of these platforms to get things taken down.

The purpose of that law has been stretched beyond recognition. If you issue a YouTube strike to a YouTuber on that platform, all hell breaks loose. That is an act of war because unless you’re doing it for the right purpose, they know you’re doing it to harass them.

This is a lot, right? You’re describing a new notice-and-takedown regime that isn’t based in the law. It’s based on some private company’s understanding of what a likeness is. And there are all kinds of reasons that I might want to publish a video of Donald Trump. There are all kinds of reasons that I might want to publish a video of Drake that have nothing to do with using his likeness for commercial purposes. And granting those people the ability to take things down is the same problem without, I don’t know, a federal fair use regime or some other… How do you balance that?

Great. So this is why it took three years, and it’s still going on, because what you’re saying is, first of all, you need to learn from the experience with the DMCA. It is the precedent we have, and there are obviously problems on both sides. You need to learn from those. The second is you need to create a new intellectual property right, in this case, much closer to trademark than to copyright, but still an intellectual property right, because that is, as you said, sort of the functional way of balancing that against the First Amendment. If you have an intellectual property right, you are allowed to remove it despite somebody’s other free speech rights. In this case, there’s a weird analysis of, do you have a free speech right to control how your voice is used? So it’s like free speech versus free speech.

What we did was, besides getting together with many First Amendment law scholars and trying to hammer this out, which was a fascinating learning process, by the way... I feel like I got to go back to law school and listen to these brilliant constitutional scholars talk about the tension between the First Amendment and intellectual property rights. What you have to do, if you’re going to create a takedown regime, is put in hurdles on both sides. You have to put in hurdles on the “I’m telling you to take it down” side. And there are lots of things we’ve learned from the past, like: Are we going to force a, in this case, First Amendment review before you can send the notice? What’s the punishment if you send a false notice? Is that punishment high enough to deter false notices? Then on the back end, you have to have a counter notice, right? And then how do you make sure that that works well and that it’s not too low a hurdle, but a little bit of a hurdle because you want to have balance on both sides?

And then finally you have this middle area where there’s going to be a decision about whether or not it’s a parody, for example, which many are, and the First Amendment trumps copyright when it comes to a parody. What if it’s news? And what is news anymore? What is bona fide news versus some of the stuff I’m getting when I’m scrolling? We then created this middle period where a temporary restraining order, a very quick legal action, could take place and a judge could actually decide the constitutional question — because nobody wants the platforms deciding constitutional questions. The people on both sides are like regular people. They’re not going to be making ultimate determinations. So that’s ultimately what we came down to in the NO FAKES Act, which is the name of the federal bill that’s now being pushed. And whether the balance there is perfect or not yet, I don’t know, but I do know that we learned a lot of lessons from the DMCA.

I think everybody is very aware that the First Amendment defenses are really paramount in any kind of regime like this, and that overprotecting on either side… If you overprotect on the First Amendment side, then this also applies to a girl who’s bullied in high school and some kid takes her face and sticks it on a body that’s not hers. You don’t want that staying up when it shouldn’t. On the other hand, I have the right to use your face for certain things because that’s my free speech, and it’s really funny, or I’m making a point about underlying character or something like that. So, long answer: there’s no perfect response, but this time we had OpenAI at the table, we had YouTube at the table, we had people from Public Knowledge at the table who bring a different perspective than we would bring. We ultimately got to a place where I don’t think folks are thrilled on all sides of it, but I think they think it’s progress.

It’s something. I will grant you that it’s something. It’s better than what we have now, but it’s fundamentally the creation of a new intellectual property right.

And there’s some balance there that almost certainly will track the same balance that copyright law and music have undergone over the past hundred years.

The initial debates about copyright law and music were about player pianos. We’ve built huge institutions to manage these rights.

I said I was going to talk about Google a lot, and the tension inside of Google when it comes to this is the next sort of nightmare law school hypothetical for you. Google has two conceptions of itself. YouTube describes itself as a licensing regime. There’s a lot of content on YouTube; the creators make it, and Google takes a license to it. Whether or not any creator ever gets to negotiate that license is of secondary concern. There’s a lot of music on YouTube, and the industry had to fight its way into licensing on YouTube. Music is existential for YouTube, and the industry knows it has that power. There’s a real back and forth. Maybe the only industry that has true negotiating power with YouTube is the music industry. And you can see this play out over and over and over again.

On the other side of the house, Google Search is a huge business; they will describe it to you as a fair use regime. We’re going to ingest all the photos in the world and let you search them; that’s fair use, and we’re going to litigate our way through that. We’re going to ingest all the books in the world for Google Books; that’s fair use, and we’re going to litigate that. And they have done that over and over and over again.

You can work with the people at YouTube. They’re very reasonable. Neal Mohan is a smart guy. He understands that he needs music on that platform to survive, and he will build you the tools that you’re asking for, and he has. And then the people running Google are like, “We’re just going to eat everything, and training an AI model is fair use.” And just in that one company, they are totally at odds with each other. They’re going to kill each other somehow. How do you make sense of that?

What’s so fascinating to me about YouTube is that it is the first real lesson in the arc of the DMCA. It’s where I think a technology-based company realized that even if you win the fight on intellectual property rights, if you don’t have a partnership through a license in the actual market where fans are, you are not going to have the service that fans really want. And so it was the first time that everybody realized that you can win the case but lose the business. Music is such a driver of YouTube, and I think this applies to AI too. Having pristine copies that fans really want, and then having marketing partnerships and partnerships with people who invest in and partner with creators, is what matters. Those creators have a better sense of how to create the bond between that particular artist that they’ve been living with and sort of building a brand around. They understand that, versus sitting in a cubicle in a tech company and trying to decide how to connect the fan with the artist in the best way, that the former piece is irreplaceable.

And I think YouTube realized that and developed Content ID even though they technically won in court and didn’t have to, so to speak. Now, the partnership is so ingrained that there’s a dependency, or a codependency, and that’s how it’s supposed to work. It also works because of this concept in copyright law called substitution. If what you’re creating substitutes for something else, it’s probably not fair use. That’s what we’re arguing in our case against Suno. You’re basically creating music that is competing on the same platform right next to human-made music; you can click on either one, and you’re paying the same subscription fee. And so you’re affecting the economic pool, and you’re effectively substituting in the marketplace.

This is the fourth factor of the fair use regime.

This is the fourth factor of fair use, which courts say is the most important: are you affecting the potential market for the underlying work? And if you are, that’s sort of problem number one. Google, unlike in the YouTube case under the DMCA, the case that they won in the Supreme Court was about creating an index. And the idea is if you click on it, it could lead you to the work that you might then buy. The search result doesn’t substitute for the work, but it could lead you to the work, which at the end of the day may not have the same economic consequence. It could even have a positive economic consequence. At least they would argue that.

So there’s this weird thing: Google won its case, and YouTube won its case, but YouTube formed a partnership despite the win because that’s the best fan experience. And Google didn’t because it wasn’t as related to the fan experience. The fan experience was potentially pointed to by their activity. So that’s my weird law school take on the two cases.

If you’re running Google, if you’re in charge of Google Gemini, you need all of the data in the world. That is just how it works. To make the models better and to win against OpenAI, you need more data, and all of those companies just took it. I do not think Google came to you and said, “Can we license all the music in your database even though we already have a license on YouTube?” In fact, they just scraped YouTube.

I don’t think creators have woken up to the fact that they’ve given a lot of economic value to Google and its models. The other AI companies also scraped YouTube, and Google can’t yell at them because then they will have to reveal that they scraped YouTube, and this is about as tense a fight in the AI industry as there is.

You’re going to come to Google at some point, and you’re going to say, “No, you got to pay us for that training.” I know you’re suing Suno, but that feels like a little test balloon for the people with the real money. At some point they’re going to say, “No, this training was fair use.” We’re watching this dynamic play out in dozens of these cases across the industry. How do you make sense of that? Where you’re going to say, “You’re going to kill us. If the AI models flood YouTube with fake music, you’re actually going to kill the supply of the data that you need.”

No, we’re putting it into policy. And it’s gotten to be even bigger now than in the Napster days because back then, as you said, the transition was from CDs. The streaming revolution allows “exploitation,” not in the negative sense, but in the positive sense. It allows it to last forever. Do you think that technology exists to support the policy there? You need a technology that can detect AI-generated media. I’m not sure that any of that exists or works well.

I think it exists and works well for fully generated AI music. We also do a lot of testing at the RIAA. That’s another thing that our tech team does. And then we disclose the results back to the folks who gave it to us. We won’t disclose it to anybody else, but if you want to know what our test says about your technology, we’ll tell you.

And I think when it comes to fully generated AI, there’s plenty of detection technologies out there that do a really good job. Once you get below that, time will make it better. So I don’t know that we could get into a lot of gradations right now. Deezer, very successfully, on a platform level, uses AI detection technology and then labels what it detects as fully generated AI, and it’s got a very low false positive rate.

I want to end by talking about the certifications for platinum records and so on. It’s an important part of what you do. All the artists want their little records on their wall. It’s amazing that it’s still records. One of our jokes at The Verge is also that no one knows how to count anything, that Nielsen ratings — I should probably disclose, we are now part of the company that owns Luminate, which powers the Billboard charts.

The Billboard charts and their methodology are always in flux. Actually, I think as of this year, they’re in a fight with YouTube. YouTube does not want to be counted on the Billboard charts because some methodologies are... Counting is very political.

You literally give awards for counting to a big number. That is in flux as artists gain more attention on TikTok. And I have probably heard that whole Nicole Kidman song, in fragments on TikTok. I’ve never listened to the whole thing all the way through, and I’m certainly never going to buy a copy of it. Again, all in flux, and then somewhere in there is a bunch of AI remixes, and now I’ve listened to “Pop Star” by Drake 10,000 times because Darth Vader is singing it to me.

How are you thinking about that methodology, as to who’s creating what? AI is in the mix, and we’re listening to more and more fragments to discover songs on social platforms than anything else.

I swear to God, you are in my daily meetings.

Can I just tell you? I’m happy to hear that. I drive The Verge team crazy because I’m like, the music industry is the future always. And they’re like, no one wants to listen to that. So thank you for indulging me. I appreciate you.

I mean, you have your finger on the pulse. This is exactly the type of stuff we’re thinking of. So right now we’re still in the world of 1500 streams equaling one unit, and what used to be the sale of a CD. Now it’s obviously singles-based more than albums-based, although we still do certify albums. And if you look at the chart eligibility program that is now being implemented outside of Billboard and OCC, in other places of the world, they have made rules to say, unless it’s substantially human, we are not going to allow it to be eligible for the chart because you’re literally taking the place of a human artist on number 49. Billboard, so far, will allow it, but they’ll label it.

We haven’t, because we certify albums that were released by record companies. We have yet to run into the issue of the AI slop or the machine-created piece. But as we get more into assistive AI, as creators start to use AI more, and you look at the chart eligibility of substantially human, does a stream as part of that 1500 mean that it still has to be a human stream? Yes, I’m sure that we will come down on that side.

But what does substantially human mean? I think over time that might change. It might be different three years from now than it is today. But at the core, it’s got to be in the control of the creator. And some people would argue that putting nine words into a music generator is an act of creation.

And that they are a creator, and therefore it’s the same thing. It’s not the same thing. Not saying that craft doesn’t change over time, and that in songwriting it’s also different than in producing and creating a recording. Using AI in the songwriting process may look very different than it does in the recording process, so we have to think about that too. But as far as gold and platinum go, we are still in the reliably safe zone of being able to know with a great deal of certainty exactly what we’re doing. I think the evolution over time is going to be in how creators use AI more than it will need to be in something that’s fully machine-readable or machine-created.

Well, Mitch, you’ve given us so much time. As you can tell, I’ve thoroughly enjoyed this conversation. I could keep going for hours and hours now.

Me too. I mean, honestly, this has been awesome. You freaked me out a little bit because you... It’s so great to see, though, because the stuff that you’re focused on is exactly what we are trying to grapple with every single day. And I think where we’ve come to is that we can’t pretend that there are easy answers. We can’t just react sort of in a knee-jerk way. We’ve got to try to figure out a path forward that’s going to allow the industry to change and grow. This industry’s adapted more times than any other industry in the history of our country. I mean, nobody goes from wax cylinders to AI in a hundred years. Can you imagine asking Boeing to make cars? So, a very adaptable industry.

I agree with you that we’re ahead of the game and that we are the future. We’re sort of living it. And the lessons that we learned from the music industry, we can apply to a lot of other things. So this has been awesome.

Thank you so much for being on Decoder. Really appreciate it.

Love Is Blind Star Michaella Giorgio’s Romance With Costar Revealed

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Love is Blind’s season 11 reunion revealed that Michaella Giorgio and beekeeper Matt Waldrip tested their connection out in the real world following her breakup with Rob Feeley.

After trying her hand at love sight unseen, Michaella Giorgio went into a second romance with eyes wide open.

During the Love Is Blind season 11 reunion on Nov. 8, it was revealed that after she and Rob Feeley ended their engagement, Michaella and her other in-pod connection Matt Waldrip (known as “the beekeeper”) gave their chemistry a try.

The news was revealed by Matt after host Nick Lachey asked if he ever, or still did, have feelings for Michaella.

“Man, where to start on that?” he said. “I'll just come out and say, Michaella and I did have a relationship after the experiment ended. You know, I feel genuinely lucky that such a remarkable woman found it worth her while to welcome me into her life after all of this and meet her family. You met my family.”

He added, “Ultimately, I did fall in love with you during that time, and I'm sorry that I'm the one that ended it. I genuinely wish you all the happiness and success in the world, Michaella. You're a queen, you're always going to hold a special place in my heart, for sure. And if you ever need anything—”

A heated Michaella, however, stopped the beekeeper mid-thought.

“I need nothing,” she said. “I need nothing. I need nothing. Newsflash, nothing.”

She also shared her take on what went down between them—and it may not have been all that sweet.

“Matt and I had a really great friendship that shouldn't have crossed friendship,” she said. "I think that's definitely an exaggeration of our relationship. We dated for a few weeks, and I dropped you off at your mom's house and met your mother. I don't think we were in love.”

Matt, however, said he had been in love with her, and that Michaella had accompanied the single father to Harry Potter and the Cursed Child with his family, also noting Michaella’s mother makes “mean chicken cutlets.”

“It was very meaningful to me,” he added, “so I'm sorry that you don't remember it the same way.”

Michaella then noted, “I did say I love you, but I did not say I was in love with you. We're going to be transparent: I was on mushrooms, which you knew that I took.”

Matt called her recollection of events “just a shame,” adding he was glad he didn’t bring the homemade honey he’d thought about gifting her to the reunion.

As for the connection that first ended in an engagement, but then a broken heart, Michaella shared some final words for Rob during the reunion.

“I have no regrets. I really fell in love with you,” she said. “I will love you forever, you know, and it's a shame how things turned out, but we're all good.”

Meanwhile, Rob shared his well wishes for his former fiancée.

“Everything that I said was genuine from the day I met you to even today, just looking at you, like it brings back so many memories,” he said. “And I let you down, so, I apologize for that. You deserve better, and you're definitely gonna find something better.”

He concluded, “And if you need me for anything, you can text me, call. Like you have a friend in me.”

For more on where the couples on Love is Blind stand today, read on.

Though yacht captain Blake’s romance with marketing manager Erika appeared to be smooth sailing after the pods, just one week before their wedding, the couple realized they may be in too deep due to their dramatically different lifestyles.

“I don’t want to hold you back,” Blake told Erika during the season 11 finale. “I don’t want you to be that captain’s wife on the widow walk. I don’t want to marry you and then for you to feel held hostage while I’m sailing around, gone for weeks on end. And then you’ve got a timeline with kids and everything.”

“I just think at this point, there’s too many concerns, too many questions that I can’t say yes at the altar,” she shared. “I just think the best thing to do is to leave the experiment.”

And while Erika cried over their decision, Blake had already concluded their ship had sailed.

“The experiment is done, but it’s the right move,” Blake confirmed. And when a producer asked if he ever had a love like this, he replied: “I’m not gonna answer that one…I’m feeling a little relieved, a little exhausted.”

While both Wilson and Wilnie had the times of their lives at their respective bachelor and bachelorette celebrations, the party was definitely over before they reached the altar.

In fact, they couldn’t see eye-to-eye on life's more mundane tasks, including dividing up cooking duties and household chores.

“When we got out of the pods, you got everything I promised you, plus,” Wilson told Wilnie following an off-camera disagreement, later adding, “You said it countless times, ‘I’ll do the things for you that make you happy.’ And I’ve mentioned what those things might be, but then you’ll be like, ‘But I don’t like doing that…I’m not used to doing that.’”

As for Wilnie, she couldn’t disagree more. “I don’t work in the home,” the mental health counselor explained. “I have long, emotionally draining days and whether you agree with that, when I get home, it is hard for me to always be at your level.”

Ultimately, for Wilson, there were too many issues at hand for them to tie the knot. “It’s not that you’re a bad person,” the senior compliance manager shared, “you’re a phenomenal person but maybe marriage is not for us.”

By the end, though a tearful Wilnie confirmed she still loved Wilson, she doesn’t regret their path.

Cam Halloran & Aneha Singh: To Be Determined

Once Aneha and Cam entered the real world, her hesitation to marry someone with a different background and religious beliefs —especially in the wake of her family’s disapproval—and their communication skills proved to be hurdles. And, yet, they seemed determined to scale them together.

However, once asked whether she was ready to say “I do,” the principal engineer shared that she couldn’t, telling her fiancé: “I still believe you’re my person, but I do not today. I need more time.”

As for Cam, he assured her that he completely understood. “It’s okay,” the human resource coordinator replied. “it’s a big decision. I feel like we both need to be one hundred percent confident.”

But once Aneha declared she didn’t and danced away from the altar while stressing to Cam that he was still her fiancé, he teared up watching her leave. As he later explained to his family, he simply didn’t “know what happens now.”

Shortly after, Aneha and Cam did end up meeting up one final time. And while Cam expressed that he still loved and cared for her, he needed time to process the “I don’t” of it all.

When healthcare worker Sarah started to fall for account executive John, they initially disagreed on fundamental topics such as abortion rights and vaccinations. However, despite their perspectives, they both said yes to spending their lives together, becoming the only couple to get married during the show’s 11th season.

“It’s fun looking back on the first few days ‘cause I don’t think we liked each other very much,” she shared. “And then, some people here heard the story about you maybe not being too jazzed to keep talking to me. But I love that that happened, because I think that was a catalyst for us.”

She noted, “After that, I’ve never been so sure on someone, and I had never even seen you. And then when I saw you, I felt like I’d already known you a lifetime.”

Just before the season's reunion aired, however, court documents showed Sarah filed for divorce just over a year after they tied the knot.

Even though the pair were shaping up to be frontrunners once they left the pods, Rob and Michaella’s relationship came to a screeching halt after he had two shocking public outbursts during episode 10.

After a hangout with his fellow friends went left once a bartender cut one of them off, Rob then came to the cast meet-up with another bone to pick.

"I’m going down in this thing as the biggest piece of s--t,” he yelled at the bar. “Right where Netflix wants me, buddy. Dude, they’re gonna manipulate this whole thing into something crazy. I don’t give a f--k."

In fact, his behavior amongst his castmates—in addition to other uncomfortable moments between the two of them—made Michaella think twice about completing the rest of their journey.

"I don’t deserve any of what I’m getting," she expressed through tears. "I love him. It’s just a lot. And we’re not getting married. I cannot marry this man under any circumstances."

Scott “Stew” Stewart & Jacqueline “Jax” Guelli: Split

Despite their initial chemistry in the pods, Stew and Jax fizzled out almost immediately once they met face-to-face.

However, even after ending their engagement, they’ve coming to a crystal-clear understanding as friends. In fact, Stew shared that even though Jax had turned down producers’ offer to meet up again immediately after their reveal, she then reached out on her own terms.

"It was super sincere," he previously told E! News. "She explained that it wasn't about me. It was about just the emotional overload of the previous couple weeks, and I think once we got settled back in Boston, once kind of normal life was allowed to resume, we were able to kind of catch our breaths."

Heading into their big day, Amber—who is mom to daughter Emma—expressed worry over Jordan actually saying “I do” when it came time.

But, her fears were put to rest as the two both ended up saying yes, leaving almost everyone present to shed tears, including her daughter.

However, their relationship came to an end just four months later, the former couple revealed during an emotional face-off at the March 2026 season 10 reunion.

Brittany Wicker & Devonta Anderson: Split

Right before their nuptials, season 10's Brittany revealed she was “shocked” when Devonta called it all off.

“Today was supposed to be our wedding day but it’s not,” she told the camera. “But right now, I feel like everything I want isn’t happening.”

As for her now ex, though Devonta noted that everything between then was “great,” ultimately, “I can’t walk into a marriage with self-doubt of ‘Can I love you how you need to be loved?’ I think that’s why I couldn’t get to the altar. I just can’t do it.”

And though Brittany walked away noting that they’re “still going to be together,” Devonta confirmed he’s not on the same page.

Indeed, Devonta revealed at the March 2026 reunion that he is now engaged to an old friend and the two are expecting a baby. The pair welcomed son Kyler Lee Anderson in August 2026.

Brianna “Breezy” McNees & Connor Spies: Split

Though season 10 star Bri decided she and Connor weren’t ready to get married just days before their wedding, at the show’s March 2026 reunion, the pair confirmed they’re still very much in love and working on eventually getting to their happily ever after.

But their journey reached a hard stop weeks later, Connor sharing on the Table for 1 podcast in June, "I'm not dating Bri anymore."

The season 10 couple's engagement came to an abrupt end once Chris confessed to Jessica that he wasn't physically attracted to her.

However, Jessica has since found love in another pod mate, as she revealed at the March 2026 reunion, with fellow doctor Haramol Gill.

"You are everything, Jess," Haramol told her during the special. "Every room you walk into gets brighter."

Though the season 10 pair nearly exchanged vows after falling in love sight unseen, their differing perspectives on having kids—Emma has voiced her uncertainty while Mike has been firm about having them—was too much of a factor to compromise on.

“I do love you,” Mike told Emma. “Right now, I’m not ready to say I do. I want kids. And I want to know that you definitely, 100 percent want a family with me. I don’t wanna say yes and jump into something and then it changes down the road.”

And though Emma noted she can “see glimpses of a family,” in their future, Mike shared that he wants to her to be certain.

"If we’re gonna get married, I want you to know that you want a family with me for sure,” he explained. “I don’t want it to be half-ass…And you have your reasons and I respect them and respect them but I just can’t say yes without knowing that. So, I’m sorry."

Mike has since hard-launched a new romance.

Before walking down the aisle in the season 10 finale, the soccer coach compared his wedding day to a “penalty shootout with everything on the line,” noting that he was confident that they would make it.

However, Ashley made it clear that it wouldn’t be the goal and jumped in when he was presented with whether he would say “I do.”

"I know what you’re going to say," she told him. "I know you don’t choose me and I’m not choosing you either.” Ashley went on to list a number of reasons why they didn’t work out, including questions about his integrity. A point Alex made sure to address in that very same moment.

"I do disagree to an extent about the integrity," he told her. "I felt as though the questions about my past were more to try and catch me in something than really understand me."

And if you’re wondering whether he would do anything differently: "Absolutely no regrets on my end, I’m so pleased and proud of myself as a person."

Victor "Vic" St. John & Christine Hamilton: Married

From the start of season 10, Christine and Vic were instantly a match made in pod heaven, with the two both happily agreeing to their future together.

"I never thought I would fall in love in such a unique experiment, and Christine,” he noted during his vows, “the moment we spoke in those pods, we just connected…You know more about me than anyone else on this planet."

She marked his 35th birthday in September with an Instagram tribute to "the kindest, most hardworking, humble, strong, gentle, passionate, joyful, God-fearing man."

In the end, Anton's drinking habits and financial situation didn't seem to match what Ali was seeking.

"I can't be your wife," she told the truck driver at the altar in season nine. "The person I fell in love with in the pods isn't the person I've gotten to experience in real life."

"It just didn't feel right or how I would expect that moment to feel like," she told the cameras. "I don't feel he was super forthcoming about his lifestyle."

Anton's reaction? "I don't know if it's looks, I don't know if it's the vibes or I don't know if it's the way that we were living together. But I can say I've done nothing but care for her."

Though the couple bonded over having loved ones with Type 1 diabetes, their differing lifestyles (he preferred a more simplistic way of life; she loves luxury) were a breaking point for Megan, who called off the wedding at the end of the season nine finale.

"We're ships in the night, like two different people," the entrepreneur told him. But the metaphor passed him by, with Jordan insisting, "I don't see it."

When Megan cited his picky eating habits and his inflexible travel schedule as a dad, Jordan told her, "I don't know why you chose me in the pods then."

"Initially I leaned into opposites attract but I still just have reservations," she explained before they each began crying. "Knowing this today, there's no way I wanted to go to the altar and put either of us through that."

Jordan shared his one regret was introducing her to his son, Luca, saying in a confessional, "I feel like she doesn't love me for me and that's the closure I need."

Megan admitted the ordeal had her "questioning if I'm even cut out to be a mom. Maybe I am too caught up in my own life."

But at the October 2025 reunion, she revealed she and partner Paul Wegman had welcomed a son they named Brooks.

Kalybriah Haskin & Edmond L. Harvey: Split

They both confessed they "love the hell out of" each other at the altar, but season nine's Kalybriah turned Edmond down with an apology.

"You deserve someone that is 100 percent at the altar and I'm not 100 percent right now," she told the realtor. Still, she stressed, "My no doesn't mean that I don't love you."

While Edmond broke down in tears, Kalybriah thanked him for being "so graceful" as she made the "hard" decision. When he asked if she'd date him "down the road," Kalybriah said she'd never write him off.

"I am shocked," he said in a confessional of her decision. "There was no bright side."

The pair didn't make it to the altar in season nine, as Joe ended the relationship after having a breakdown at his tux fitting.

"On and off camera, Joe was different," Madison later told Glamour. "Off camera, he was far more reassuring. We were very intimate, very touchy. And then on camera, it was the opposite, and you kind of see that at the pool party."

Madison even asked Joe about his attraction for her, which he confessed was an issue during their vacation.

"I kind of dug deeper, like, 'What is your typical type?' And he was like, 'I just usually date more petite women,'" she recalled to the outlet. "I am a really confident person. At that moment, it kind of put me into this insecure feeling."

Joe defended his comment, telling the outlet, “I was trying, poorly, to explain a pattern in my past dating experiences, not to compare or criticize."

Nick dumped Annie before the wedding day in season nine, explaining he was hurt that she continued to say she loved him more than he loved her.

However, the hairstylist refused to believe it was over and tried to convince Nick to continue, saying, "Just be in this and love me."

"You can't make one plus one equal three," he said, prompting her to say, "Watch me."

Nick ultimately told Annie, “The reason that I am not willing to marry you is because I am unhappy."

Lauren O'Brien and David Bettenburg: Split

Their romance failed to take off once the season eight pair landed in Honduras, with Dave revealing he and his then-fiancée had mutual friends in common. But it wasn’t the similar inner circles that proved to be the problem, but rather, Dave’s hesitation in moving forward after they told him about Lauren hooking up with someone they knew before the show.

Not to mention, Dave also didn’t want to introduce Lauren to his friends and family, noting that his dad didn’t know of his engagement and that his sister—who became a big point of contention—disapproved of their relationship altogether.

One of the most compatible couples of season eight, most viewers assumed Monica and Joey would make it down the aisle and beyond. However, when it came to saying "I do," Monica instead said, "I don’t right now."

"In this moment, it's 100 percent," she said through tears, "and I just feel like we're both, we're not quite there." As for Joey, he agreed with every word.

“That's totally okay,” he explained after hugging her. “I think we both have a tremendous amount of love for each other, and that's apparent. But I think there's bigger things and bigger growth. And I agree, I don’t think I would be ready. And to be completely honest, I’m also not 100 percent at this time.”

Though season eight's Ben and Sara seemed to disagree on major components of their life together—including religion and politics—by wedding day, the pair seemed to have gotten on the same page. But ultimately, they decided to end their shared story.

As Sara—who said, "I don’t" to a future with Ben—explained, "Ben, I love you so much but I always wanted a partner to be on the same wavelength, and so today, I can't. I'm sorry but I don't want that to be misunderstood."

She later added, "I know that the connection we have is so real and my heart is there, but when we talked about a lot of the values I hold so close to my heart, making this decision, my mind is telling me I can't."

Another season eight couple who seemed to be focused on the road ahead post-pods, Devin and Virginia, got along perfectly fine—that is, until the topic of having a prenup came up.

But it was even bigger issues that proved to be enough for Virginia, leading her to ultimately saying no to spending the rest of her life with Devin.

“I think that in a lot of our relationship, I wanted to protect Devin,” she said, through tears, after the leaving the altar. “And I don't know if that's a cultural thing, but it's always been important for me to protect him. So, there were even topics that he didn't want to talk about, like politics, there were just certain things he just didn't want me to push on. So there was just depths of our relationship that we didn't get to. We just didn't get there.”

There was at least one season eight couple who did say yes to a season of love past Christmas.

Though the pair seemed to face quite the hurdle (Taylor was convinced Daniel followed, then unfollowed her on Instagram before the show and therefore, knew what she looked like), they managed to clear that particular obstacle.

In fact, after exchanging emotional vows, the two enjoyed a beautiful wedding and celebratory reception.

"We did it," Daniel said while sitting alongisde Taylor after their nuptials, with Taylor adding, "We're so excited to start the next chapter." They marked two years of their "wild journey" with a March 2026 Instagram post.

Brittany Wisniewski and Leo Braudy: Split

Though the now-famed art dealer decided to pop the question to the Keeping Well author following his season seven love triangle with Hannah Jiles, the pair ultimately decided after their short engagement, they were better off as friends.

“The chemistry just wasn't there,” Brittany previously told People in a joint interview with Leo. “We had a talk in the beginning about how this could work and then time went on during the trip and the chemistry just wasn't there and we mutually felt that. We're very good communicators, so it was not a big ordeal. It was a very clean, respectful realization.”

Shortly after her appearance on the Netflix dating show, Brittany debuted her romance with musician Mod Sun in November 2024, but she confirmed in July 2025 that the pair had called it quits.

Monica Davis and Stephen Richardson: Split

Monica and Stephen’s relationship came to a screeching halt in season seven when she uncovered “really kinky” sext messages sent between her fiancé and another woman, causing the pair to break up shortly after they arrived home from Mexico. As for Stephen, he apologized in the moment during episode eight, but he also shared a message on social media once the episode aired.

"I hope I didn't ruin anyone's day by existing," he wrote on Instagram Stories in October 2024, "and if I did, I'm sorry you had to wake up this morning. I wish you didn't have to either. But I'll love you all the same."

Once Hannah and Nick embarked on life outside the pods, the season seven pair had their fair share of ups and downs—and disagreements—to say the least. (Let’s not forget: “I think you get treated as an equal when you star contributing like an equal.”)

And it all came to a head when Hannah decided they wouldn’t make it down the aisle. As for Nick, he called their breakup “one of the worst feelings of my life.”

Since then, Hannah has admitted the show forced her to do some self-introspection.

“I’ve learned a lot, I’ve learned there’s things I need to work on,” she said during an October 2024 episode of The Viall Files podcast. “For example, I am a very direct person. I know that but I think my delivery is rough. And I’ve learned that delivery is everything.”

Taylor Krause and Garrett Josemans: Married

A match made in science-based heaven (i.e. those serendipitous tattoos), season seven stars Taylor and Garrett clicked from the very beginning and just seemed destined to say, “I do.” Though they did hit a rough patch once Taylor found out he responded to an ex’s DM (according to Garrett, he confirmed he was engaged and shut it down), the rest is Love Is Blind history.

"I'm very grateful to have a life partner like my husband because no matter where we are in the world you make it feel like home," Taylor wrote in a November 2025 Instagram post marking their second anniversary. "I admire how much we have grown to build a life together and much of that has been because of you."

Yes, you read that right: Though Marissa and Ramses rose to become one of season seven's frontrunners of the couples who would make it, he ultimately called off their wedding after having second thoughts.

“We have a great future ahead of us, I want to marry you,” Marissa told her fiancé after a sit-down to which he responds, “I understand falling in love is a risk, I understand that.”

However, he noted their differences—which have included his stance on the military—may be too tough to work through. “After being married and seeing other marriages and hearing from other married people,” he added. “I just don’t think love alone is enough…I think there needs to be other things that are also working.”

As for Marissa, though she tried to navigate past their perspectives, once she confirmed it was over, she broke down into tears. “I don’t think you should’ve come to this experience,” she tells him. “How could I feel so sure? And you’re not? It sucks, I want you to choose me,” adding of their split, “This is the worst day of my life.”

She announced her marriage to former partner D. Johnson in May 2026.

Tyler Francis and Ashley Adionser: Divorced

Tyler and Ashley quickly became a fan-favorite couple after season seven viewers watched their relatively easy-going romance unfold. However, the couple hit a bump in the road once Tyler shared that he had three children, which Ashley referred to as his "sperm donor babies." (However, there’s online discourse over the extent of his involvement, which he has not addressed publicly).

By the October 2024 reunion episode, Ashley and Tyler were able to move past this fact and went on to not only reconnect—but in fact, become husband and wife.

“We started this journey two months ago and this changed our entire lives,” Tyler says while sitting alongside Ashley after their wedding. “Now, we get to spend the rest of our lives together.”

However, Ashley later shared that the pair had called it quits following over one year of marriage.

"After much reflection, I want to share that Tyler and I have been separated for several weeks and have now made the difficult decision to end our marriage," she told People in a January 2025 statement. "While I had hoped for mutual understanding and transparency in our relationship, it has become clear that our paths are no longer aligned, making it impossible for me to continue in this marriage."

Season seven stars Alex and Tim seemed to smooth over their major disagreement in Mexico by the time they got back home to Washington, D.C. In fact, Tim had an emotional meeting with Alex’s dad that seemed to cement their future.

However, after Alex met with Tim’s family, they got into yet another argument and Tim ended their engagement by telling Alex that he didn’t ever want to see her again. Since then, both have spoken out to defend themselves after the breakup, with Alex noting that their split was not “a mutual decision.”

GTA 6 party continues with Travis Scott, Cardi B, and more while you’ll be at home playing the game

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Real world parties for an open world game.

It’s hard to believe, but Grand Theft Auto 6 is finally coming out next week, and Rockstar Games and PlayStation are throwing another big party in its honor.

Today, the companies announced GTA 6: The Festival, a special live concert at a convention center in Miami that’s “featuring a star-studded lineup of artists from the game and the official soundtrack,” including Travis Scott, Cardi B, Metro Boomin, and more, like French DJ duo Justice, who scored the game’s soundtrack.

Grand Theft Auto VI: The Festival, a special live event on November 21 at Mana Wynwood in Miami to celebrate the launch of GTAVI, featuring a star-studded lineup of artists from the game and the official soundtrack.More info: https://t.co/XRlfYFOk0H pic.twitter.com/fQyOU7I4gU

“This is a one-of-a-kind launch event that features two stages of nonstop music,” the website teases, confirming that entrants need to be 17 or older, and getting tickets means winning a literal lottery that will be drawn tomorrow at 6pm CT.

This sounds fun, and I’m sure it’ll be a great time, but I’m not really sure who the target demographic is for this sort of event. It’s definitely not GTA 6 players, right? Because when the event takes place next Saturday, Nov. 21, we’ll all be in Vice City playing through Jason and Lucia’s story as opposed to clubbing it up. Plus, can’t we just go to nightclubs in the game instead, and not have to deal with brain-pounding music, sweating profusely, and weird smells all around us?

I shouldn’t speak for all gamers, but I feel like I’m justified in saying that. GTA fans have been waiting over 13 years for this game, so while one night out feels like nothing in comparison, I think we’d all rather be playing the game itself, especially on its first weekend.

This is just another wildly outrageous form of advertising for GTA 6, which already has takeovers planned for the Miami Heat game on Nov. 18, the E11EVEN Miami nightclub turning into its Vice City counterpart, NINE1NINE Vice City, for a whole month, and a whole lot more.

You can read more about GTA 6: The Festival on Rockstar Games’ website, which is also filled with a slew of awesome screenshots of the actual game.

IRSA Inversiones (IRS) Q1 2027 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Currency-driven loss hides a 20% jump in dollar rental EBITDA.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

IRSA Inversiones y Representaciones Sociedad Anónima (IRS +1.35%) reported a net loss for the first quarter of fiscal year 2027 as Argentine inflation outpaced the rate of currency devaluation, impacting the fair value of its investment properties. Management reported that despite the currency-driven loss, the rental segment achieved a 20% increase in EBITDA when measured in U.S. dollars. The company is currently executing a $150 million capital expenditure plan, which executives identified as one of its most significant investment cycles in a decade. This program focuses on expanding the shopping mall portfolio, revitalizing the Edificio Del Plata residential project, and advancing the large-scale Ramblas del Plata mixed-use development.

Top 10 Resorts in the East for Snow Quality (2027)

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

On the East Coast, skiing is about more than great lines and great snow. It’s SUVs trudging through storms with their ski racks, salt stains, and “SKI THE EAST” bumper stickers. The snow is firmer, the peaks are lower, and terrain is more dense, but no less breathtaking. Mostly, though, it’s about skiing not as a hobby but as an identity—something you do because you’ve always done it, and always done it here.

Maybe you’ve heard that the East Coast has a reputation for its “ice.” For much of the region, that’s an outdated stereotype. Sure, these spots might not get the massive powder days that some of the Western resorts are known for, but with modern innovations in snowmaking (and a few good breaks from Mother Nature), the East can more than hold its own. After all, anyone in their right mind would have chosen and East Coast winter over the West’s sad season last year.

If you’re looking for the East Coast resorts that typically get the best snow, the 10 resorts listed below are a fine place to begin. These choices, which come from SKI’s Annual Reader Resort Survey, are known for serving up the most powder days and the best snow conditions all season long.

Farewell to Richard Lloyd, the Wild Guitar Boy at the Heart of Television

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The music world is in mourning Richard Lloyd, the guitar virtuoso from Television, the New York band that put CBGB on the map and kicked off the punk rock explosion. Lloyd made Television one of the all-time great guitar bands, in his tumultuous partnership with Tom Verlaine. He died on Saturday, at 75, after a fight with cancer. But he created a lifetime’s worth of rock magic with Television, in classics like “Marquee Moon,” “Little Johnny Jewel,” and “Days.”

Television barely stuck together for five years and two albums — yet that was enough to change how guitar music has sounded ever since. They never got a shot at American radio airplay, but they inspired upstarts all over the world, like R.E.M., U2, Sonic Youth, and Radiohead. Lloyd played so many of their most dazzling solos, as in “Elevation,” “Guiding Light,” or “See No Evil,” where his guitar sums up all the “destructive urges” Verlaine sings about. You could devote your whole life to exploring Television live bootlegs, and some of us do.

Lloyd was always underrated — the George Harrison figure in Television, a couple of years younger than Verlaine, but constantly rebelling against his kid-brother status. He was the band’s wild child, the chaotic rock & roll hellion with the face of a choirboy. Verlaine didn’t touch drugs; Lloyd gobbled them up. Verlaine hated bars and clubs; Lloyd hated leaving. As he recalled in his memoir, “I called myself a try-sexual at the time, because I would try anything a couple of times to see if I liked it. For a while, I supported myself as a male prostitute, just like Dee Dee Ramone, around the corner of 53rd and 3rd.”

Television weren’t built to last — not with these volatile personalities. “I’m already a manic depressive – bipolar A, as they say,” Lloyd told Guitar World earlier this year. “Every time we went into the studio, Tom would literally drive me nuts. He would act in such a way that I would end up in the mental hospital, or just the regular hospital.” Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

But nobody — except maybe Verlaine — could miss the spectral beauty of their twin-guitar chemistry. Together, they made the timeless 1977 classic Marquee Moon. With Lloyd on Strat and Verlaine on Jazzmaster, they plugged into the urban grime of New York City and came up with a whole new kind of psychedelic punk. Lloyd was Bob Weir to Verlaine’s Jerry Garcia, Phife Dawg to his Q-Tip, Carl Wilson to his Brian, with the tough rhythm section of Fred Smith and Billy Ficca giving both axemen room to stretch out. Smith died this past February, Verlaine in 2023, leaving Ficca as the last man standing.

Lloyd was a New York kid who grew up in Greenwich Village, at the height of the Sixties party. By all accounts, he jumped right in, prowling the city for sex and drugs and adventure. He also faced mental health issues, getting locked up in various institutions, given chemical shock treatment.

But Lloyd had a superhuman knack for charming people, with his magnetic looks and personality, talking his way into any room. As he recalled, “I was a vagabond, but a pretty one.” He hit the Fillmore East to see the Grateful Dead or Led Zeppelin or the Allman Brothers, the kind of guy who could always get in free. He was brazen enough to walk in on a Dead soundcheck and ask Jerry Garcia to put him on the guest list that night. As Lloyd told the tale, Phil Lesh was the one who said yes.

He was always a wild thing. In his highly entertaining 2018 memoir Everything Is Combustible, he recalls an incident from his teen years, when he was in Central Park but suddenly decided he wanted to move to another planet. So he walked all the way to the World Trade Center, 120 blocks or so—barefoot. As he wrote, “I found elevators that eventually took me up to the 106th floor. I entered an office and asked where the World Trade Center was. That is, where was the trading center where I could change worlds?” Related Content Richard Lloyd, Television's Virtuoso Guitarist, Dead at 75 Television Guitarist Richard Lloyd Diagnosed With Bile-Duct Cancer Watch the First Teaser for ‘Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story’ Melissa Gilbert Urges Stage Parents to ‘Be Sure’ It’s What Child Wants in Wake of Daveigh Chase Death

The question didn’t go over well. “As I found out, they also had jail cells on the 103rd floor, one of which they put me in.” They took him to Bellevue — not his first mental ward, and not his last.

He rambled from Boston to L.A., returning to NYC in 1973 with platinum-blonde hair, to catch his new favorite band, the glam-punk pioneers in the New York Dolls. He knew how to slither into any scene, immediately making himself the prettiest kid there. He became a regular at Max’s Kansas City, scamming the free chicken wings and finding lovesick older men to offer him places to stay. One of these men was Terry Ork, an Andy Warhol cohort who managed the bookstore Cinemabilia, where the employees included future punk legends Tom Verlaine, Richard Hell, and Robert Quine.

One night, Ork took him to an open-mic night to see Verlaine play solo. As soon as he sang “Venus,” Lloyd knew they belonged in a band together. Verlaine had already formed the Neon Boys with his high-school friend and fellow poet Richard Hell, but Lloyd was the real neon boy that they were looking for, the flashy brat, the agent of chaos in a band full of uptight control freaks. Lloyd made them a real rock & roll band instead of an art project.

Television found a place to play in March 1974 — a sleazy Bowery dive bar called CBGB. “Tom and I were walking down from his house to the loft in Chinatown,” Lloyd recalled in Clinton Heylin’s essential punk history From the Velvets to the Voidoids, “and we passed this place which the owner was outside fixing up. We asked if maybe we could play there—and he told us he was going to call the place Country, Bluegrass, and Blues. So we said, ‘Yes, we play stuff like that.’”

A ridiculous claim — but somehow, people always seemed eager to believe anything Richard Lloyd told them. Hilly Kristal called their bluff, and suddenly Television had a weekly residency. They played CBGB every Sunday night, drawing 20 or 30 people, until word began to spread. David Bowie came by in May, and declared them “the most original band I’ve seen in New York. They have it.” They drew stars like Lou Reed, Paul Simon, and director Nick Ray, who called them “four cats with a passion.” The NYC punk scene blew up at CBGB, with rebels like the Ramones, Patti Smith, Blondie, and Talking Heads. As Lloyd boasted in 2007, “CBGBs is the most famous rock & roll club to have ever existed and I fucking created it!”

Patti Smith put Television on the map with her famous Rock Scene review in 1974, before the band even had a record out. “Richard Lloyd plays emotional and highly sexually aware guitar,” she wrote. “He’s the pouty boyish one. The one most likely to get beat up in a parking lot.” Smith totally nailed the mystique of the band as “a movement of inspired mutants that will take the slop out of rock.”

But Tom Verlaine and Richard Hell battled for control of the band, until Hell quit and Fred Smith took over on bass. His chops let Television develop into the jam-till-dawn groove monsters they were born to be, stretching out into double-digit minutes for twin-guitar epics like “Marquee Moon” and “Friction.” They cut their debut indie single in 1975, with a tame studio version of “Little Johnny Jewel,” barely hinting at its onstage power. Lloyd hated it so much he quit for three days.

Television made two great 1970s studio albums: Marquee Moon is the universally acclaimed classic, while Adventure is the wrongly underrated follow-up. (As the zine guru Byron Coley once wrote, “Marquee Moon for songs, Adventure for playing.”) Instead of splitting into conventional lead/rhythm roles, they traded off solos. (Verlaine, prickly as always, made sure the album credits itemized which ones he played.) Lloyd brought the melodic sparkle of their succinct pop tunes, like “Venus” and “Days.” But he also brought the urgent rhythmic drive — Verlaine always needed a guitar partner to give his music a kick in the ass, and he never had one who kicked like Lloyd. You can hear that in fiery live documents like The Blow Up, with their famous destruction of Bob Dylan’s “Knockin’ on Heaven’s Door.”

There’s nothing like the magic these guys conjured up onstage. To hear Richard Lloyd at his best, go to the summer of 1978, when they already knew they were breaking up, but playing the most ferocious guitar fireworks of their lives. Check out the 12-minute “Little Johnny Jewel” from the San Francisco gig, finally released as Live at the Old Waldorf — a blowout that feels like a defiant last stand. Or the 17-minute “Marquee Moon” from Portland, a few nights later. Or the 13-minute “Kingdom Come” from back in NYC on July 29. It’s downright bizarre that so much of their greatest work remains officially unreleased.

But in any version, “Marquee Moon” is their zenith, the mutant love-child of “Dark Star” and “Sister Ray,” an epic trip into the unknown that wandered somewhere different every night. Lloyd delivered the garage-band propulsion, while Verlaine aimed for the interstellar jazz space of John Coltrane or Albert Ayler. “Marquee Moon” has been covered by bands from Pavement to Geese to Wilco — none of them trying to match the original, just to live up to that adventurous spirit.

Television never sold diddly in the U.S., but they made a worldwide splash. “Marquee Moon” became an unlikely hit single in the U.K, making the Top 30, while they also made the charts with “Prove It” and “Foxhole.” Adventure was a Top Ten album there, proving this band had a bright Eighties future. But sadly, Television were already falling apart, and so was Lloyd — a “full-blown junkie,” by his own account. They broke up in 1978, at the peak of their musical powers.  Trending Stories Watch Ariana Grande, Ben Stiller, Robert De Niro Take Over ‘The Tonight Show’ U2 Sound Revitalized on ‘Carnaval de Luz’ Del Casher, Guitarist Who Invented the Wah-Wah Pedal, Dead at 88 Russell Brand Sentenced to More Than 10 Years in Prison After Rape Conviction

Lloyd moved on to sharp solo albums like 1979’s Alchemy and 1986’s Field of Fire. He also wrote and co-produced the dBs’ 1978 debut single, “I Thought You Wanted To Know.” Television’s short-lived 1992 reunion resulted in a low-key self-titled album, with Lloyd shining in gems like “1880 or So” and “No Glamour for Willi.” (They played sporadic shows in the 2000s, but Lloyd left for good in 2007.) He joined the Cleveland proto-punks Rocket from the Tombs, in the place of their original guitarist Peter Laughner— ironic, since Laughner was the guy who briefly replaced him in Television in 1975. He was a longtime foil for the ace Nineties singer-songwriter Matthew Sweet, especially on his 1991 breakthrough Girlfriend, where the other lead guitarist was another CBGB legend, Robert Quine. Lloyd brought his unique sound to “Divine Intervention,” “I’ve Been Waiting,” “Falling,” and the 1995 MTV hit “Sick of Myself.” In the video, Lloyd plays the solo from his hospital bed, after crashing his car.

But he cleaned up and kept gigging the rest of his life — the fact that he lived to be 75 is one of his most surprising achievements. As he shrugged in his memoir, “I’ve been clinically dead a couple times, so what difference does it make to me? I like experience.” In 2002, Television reunited for their first NYC gigs in a decade. I’ll never forget the boyish mischief all over his face as he ripped through evergreens like “Venus,” “O Mi Amore,” and a wonderfully trippy “Marquee Moon.” That sense of mischief and adventure lives on in everything Richard Lloyd ever played — and that’s why his guiding light still shines on.

These Are The Worst Road Trips You've Ever Taken

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Not every road trip is an adventure that you'll remember for the rest of your life. We wish every day on the road could be sunshine and rainbows, but sometimes your vacation is just nonstop thunderstorms. God forbid you decide January is a good time to visit a national park, but the highway to get there is unplowed and has turned into a stranded-car sanctuary.

We asked our readers last week about the worst road trip they've ever taken. I honestly couldn't conceive how bad things could get when stuck inside a car with other people for hours on end. Commenters submitted some truly absurd anecdotes, ranging from being locked inside a scorching, aluminum-topped pickup bed with his brother as kids to someone's beloved Border Collie defecating in his girlfriend's lap. I love driving, but have road trips been a secret form of torture all along? Without further ado, here are the long-distance drives you'd love to forget:

Right around the turn of the century, my wife and I had to pick up our stepdaughter at Chicago Midway Airport so she could come home after spending Christmas with her bio-dad.

Nothing majorly bad happened to us, but it was a very white-knuckle drive.

A huge blizzard hit in the morning when we were supposed to leave. Bio-dad said her flight still hadn't been canceled, so we set off on our way (early, of course).

What normally would have been a 2-hour trip turned into a 4-hour one. Literally, when we got off the highway at our exit to go to Midway, he calls and says her flight was canceled.

So we turned around and headed back home. That was a 6-hour trip going about 30 MPH the whole way. That was the worst because I was really tired by then. It was dark by that point, so you really couldn't see anything but the snow coming down. My mind kept seeing cars spin out in front of me, when they weren't.

We made it almost all the way home but got stuck in the middle of the road about 3 miles from home. A nice couple was there in a 4X4 when it happened, so they gave us a push and that got us home.

We did get stuck in the driveway, but that was something to deal with the next day.

I never want to drive 10+ hours in a blizzard ever again!

In the days of paper maps, was skiing with my girlfriend in Vermont, and we planned to drive to a town about 10 miles away for a nice dinner (jacket and tie, candlelight, etc.). Driving along, some roads plowed, some not, but no biggie. Start driving up a hill, encouraged by the fact that, although there was snow on the road, there were tracks, and I had 4WD. As the snow started getting deeper, my brain clicked: those are snowmobile tracks. Stopped the car, did a 34-point turn to make sure I stayed on the road and didn't go into a ditch or a gully, and coasted back down the hill. Ended up grabbing pizza right near the place we were staying.

Around 2017, driving my wife's new-to-us BMW with runflats to Blacksburg, VA from CT. Car was CPO and had bout 20K miles. We hit a mega hole on I-78 in NJ near the PA line. Two passenger-side tires blew. Torrential rain, 5 a.m. could not see a thing. Pulled over and found a BMW dealer 20 miles away in PA that would open in a couple of hours. Went there and waited. The service manager was great, he looked the car over and told us that the remaining tires were new and that changing just two was not a problem. Three months later, the front diff fails. $6700 estimate, a small battle with BMW corporate, but once we produced the service doc with the dealer's recommendation of only two tires they fixed the car under the warranty. Thing is, they did a repair, not a replace and sure enough the thing fails again just out of warranty. Traded it in for an Accord. A word of caution: NEVER buy an AWD, especially German, if you don't know the tire replacement history. Essentially, tires need to be replaced in fours unless the others are very fresh. My trusted indy tells me that as little as 9mm can make the difference!!!!

We drove from Charleston, SC to the Outer Banks of NC in August in a 1989 Jeep Wrangler with no AC in the rain. The windshield kept fogging up and the road conditions were awful. We had to make time to meet the ferry schedule and it was sweaty, back pain-filled, bouncy 9 hours...

Amarillo to Dallas for Christmas break in school. My Mazda3 hatch was loaded to the gills with everything we needed to bring home during the break, plus our snowboard gear. A small spot was carved out for our Border Collie to sit. Unbeknownst to me, my girlfriend (now wife) had fed him 6 egg yolks from her egg white omelet that morning and had given him terrible gas. We stopped often to get him to poop, but nothing. Then, in the final 5 miles to her parents' home, he panicked in the backseat. We were on an under-construction part of highway with no exits in sight and no shoulder when he jumped from the back seat into the front seat (he is now properly secured while in the car). I'm trying my best not to crash while looking for an exit while she grabs him and bear hugs him to herself. I then hear her say, "he's sh*tting, he's sh*tting," as she holds him tight and has diarrhea all into her lap. I pull into a Taco Bueno parking lot and she takes him out. I go in and get cleaning supplies and we clean him up, throw away her snuggy-like robe, and put him in a trash bag with his head sticking out for the 10-minute drive to her parents' house. The only casualty was the passenger seat belt, which she accidentally let fully retract and required a full deep clean/disassemble and a 48-hr air out.

Houston to Chicago and back in a brand new BEV. Having spent 15 years in PHEVs, expected refueling to be a minor issue. It was a major issue; chargers were often inaccessible, broken, vandalized, few and far between. Yes, one can spend a tremendous amount of time gaming out a plan for slightly better results, but lotza time and only slightly better results. The stalling out of the plans to make electric cars viable, by the Republicans, cost manufacturers TONS of money and great frustration for me personally.

"Of all the sad words of tongue or pen, the saddest of these: it might have been." (if not for the lingering orange fart in the White House)

May 2019. 7 friends, 4 guys and 3 girls on a 2-week road trip from New Orleans to Yosemite and back. At least that was the plan. Our last hurrah trip before graduating college didn't go right from the start. Imagine 7 adults in a rented Escalade PLUS 2 weeks' worth of gear! We overpacked from the start. We had to leave all our tents behind except for one, which was my friend's dad's old Boy Scout tent that smelled like toe cheese and had old wax coating peeling off. We left late because of the packing, so we were constantly behind schedule as a result. We also overplanned things to do so this meant we'd see something for 5 minutes and then have to keep driving so we wouldn't miss something else! Texas went on forever. The big crater in Arizona? Didn't get to see it, had to drive right by. The Grand Canyon? We got there late in the day instead of the morning, so instead of hiking and seeing it we got to look at it for 30 minutes, 15 of which were obscured by clouds. Then we got sleeted on and had to leave. Vegas? Giant waste of time where we all secretly confessed to looking at flights home and tried to salvage the trip... and friendships. Drove through Death Valley in the middle of the night. Tensions were high. Some people were controlling. There were tons of fights and crying. It was too cold to camp so we diverted to San Diego for a few days which saved us. Then Santa Fe. Then 20 hours through storms in Texas and then home. Too many people and too much time. Never again.

The past two summers, I've taken my 1979 El Camino from northern Wisconsin to be a part of Elcofest in Indiana the final weekend in July with no A/C. Both times it was 95ºF, muggy, and rainy, so we had to keep the windows up. For me, those were among the best road trips I've ever taken. But if you ask the wife, she'd probably say they were among the worst.

In the late '70s, I drove from Saint Paul up to northern Minnesota in -10 degrees to go cross-country skiing. Unfortunately, the heat in my rusted-out VW Squareback was not up for the trip. My passenger rode in a down sleeping bag. We had to constantly scrape the ice off the inside of the windows and windshield. My passenger refused to ride back with me, instead opting for the bus from Bemidji.

Ah, Yes. The old air-cooled VW winter experience, all bundled up with one gloved hand on the wheel, and the other with an ice scraper!

Indiana to John Pennecamp State Park in the Florida Keys. In summer. My brother and I rode in the bed of my dad's truck (a single-cab F-100) under a beercan-thin aluminum topper for ALL the miles. Occasionally one of us would be allowed to go through the rear sliding window and sit in the cab with mom and dad. We could open the windows to the topper, but then it got REALLY loud, so we had to use that sparingly. We mostly just baked.

Bonus points: we were sitting on a plywood platform covered in that 1970s green outdoor grass carpet, and under the platform were several scuba tanks which we had been told were full and super dangerous and could explode. Because 1970s parents thought that sort of thing was hilarious.

It was hot, loud, uncomfortable and we spent the whole time going down afraid for our lives. It was years later that our parents explained that the tanks were empty; they just wanted us not to move around too much.

Waymo robotaxis kept illegally passing school buses after software fix, feds say

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Nearly a year ago, Waymo updated its self-driving software and issued a recall after numerous reports that its robotaxis were illegally passing school buses that had stopped and deployed flashing red lights. Now, federal safety regulators have opened a new investigation into why that behavior has continued.

The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration said in a November 6 filing that a majority of these reported incidents occurred after the company deployed a software update intended to fix the problem.

Reuters was the first to report the filing.

Waymo said last year that a software update was completed for all affected vehicles on November 17, 2025. However, NHTSA said the majority of the incidents occurred after the software update. NHTSA’s Office of Defects Investigation (ODI) has opened a “recall query” to “assess the adequacy of the remedy.”

The ODI opened its initial investigation into Waymo in October 2025 after seeing footage of one of its autonomous vehicles maneuvering around a stopped school bus that had its stop sign extended, lights flashing, and was unloading children in Atlanta. In that incident, a Waymo robotaxi crossed perpendicularly in front of the school bus from its right side. The autonomous vehicle then turned left around the front of the bus before traveling down the street.

Numerous other incidents have been documented in the weeks and months since in Austin, where Waymo also operates a robotaxi service. Austin Independent School District officials reported at least five such incidents at the time, according to a letter filed with NHTSA.

TechCrunch has reached out to Waymo and Austin Independent School District officials for comment and will update the article if they respond.

Activision cracking down on popular Call of Duty: Warzone app that basically constituted cheating

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

"Lobby shopping" is what Call of Duty: Warzone players have been calling the ability to check the skill level of players in a lobby prior to joining it, enabling them to pass on those that include a lot of tough competition.

There are several ways players, notably Twitch streamers and content creators, have been able to do that over the years. But the use of one app in particular - Warzone Agent - has been considered by many to be cheating, and Activision is finally doing something about it.

Alongside the launch of Modern Warfare 4's version of Warzone and the game's first season of post-launch content, Call of Duty's anti-cheat team Ricochet has announced it's officially cracking down on Warzone Agent.

"Yesterday, we began taking action against Warzone Agent, a paid, unauthorised third party tool that lets users lobby shop in Warzone," the developer revealed in a post on X. "Ricochet anti-cheat will block players attempting to load the software and instruct them to unload it before accessing multiplayer."

Having the ability to cherrypick the lobby with the lowest-skilled players possible bypasses the game's matchmaking algorithm, potentially ruining the game for the dozens of other players in the match. Historically, content creators have used the app (and other methods) to post impressive kill records.

As part of this crackdown, Ricochet reiterated its stance on boosting - artificially increasing (or decreasing) one's skill rating - saying that it too is considered cheating. "Receiving a boost, having another player log in to boost your account, or purchasing a boosted account are all against policy," the developer said.

"Boosting services flood lobbies with low effort accounts used to farm XP and complete challenges. Those accounts can later be sold as Ranked Play ready, directly feeding the cheating marketplace."

More than 20,000 boosting-related accounts have been removed since the launch of Modern Warfare 4, according to the developer, and Ricochet will "continue to apply pressure" in this area.

The quality of multiplayer was a particular highlight in Eurogamer's review of Modern Warfare 4, in which Dom Peppiatt lauded the complexity of weapon handling, which works without taking away from accessibility of the gunplay.
